history, its fresh sample succeeds with probability at least \(5/12\).
Consequently, the probability of surviving \(s\) full cycles is at most
\((7/12)^s\), which tends to zero, and the expected number of cycles is at
most \(12/5\). Every child divides \(N\), so the hereditary promise applies at
every recursive node. Induction on the input value gives complete correctness,
and the geometric tail gives almost-sure termination.

\paragraph{Expected bit complexity.}
Since \(M_B(k)\mid B(k)!\),
\[
\log_2M_B(k)\le\log_2(B(k)!)
\le B(k)\log_2B(k).
\]
Iterative LCM construction uses \(B(k)-1\) GCD/multiplication steps, and every
intermediate value divides \(B(k)!\). Each
\(d=tM_B(k)\) therefore has
\[
O(B(k)\log B(k)+\log R(k))
\]
bits. One full cycle performs \(R(k)\) exact samples, modular
exponentiations, and GCD computations. The schedule functions and all these
operations cost polynomially many bit operations by hypothesis. The expected
cycle count is constant.

Exact maximal-power detection is polynomial: enumerate
\(2\le e\le k\), find the integer \(e\)-th root by binary search with products
capped above \(K\), and verify equality. Choosing the maximal exponent leaves
a base that is not a perfect power, so unary perfect-power nodes cannot be
consecutive. Charge each unary node to the following split node or prime leaf.
The binary split tree has fewer than \(2m\) nodes, and the full recursion fewer
than \(4m\) invocations. Monotonicity bounds every local schedule by its value
at \(m\); linearity of expectation yields polynomial expected total time and
polynomial space. \(\square\)

\section{Full proof of the coverage-separation barrier}

\subsection{Proof of BAR-001}\label{proof:BAR-001}

For a profile \(R=(r_1,\ldots,r_s)\), suppose \(d\) separates the profile.
There is a hit index \(i\) and a miss index \(j\), so
\(d\in\Sigma_\Delta(r_i)\) and
\(d\notin\Sigma_\Delta(r_j)\). The signatures are not all equal.
Conversely, if two signatures differ, an element of their symmetric difference
is divisible by one associated order but not the other. Its support on the
full profile is nonempty and proper. Thus a profile is separated exactly when
its signatures are not all equal.

It follows that every profile containing two distinct values from
\(\{1,\ldots,n\}\) is separated exactly when the signature map is injective
there. The \(n\)-divisor property requires only that every signature be
nonempty, so it cannot imply injectivity. The explicit counterexample
\(\Delta=\{2\}\) has
\[
\Sigma_\Delta(1)=\Sigma_\Delta(2)=\{2\}.
\]
It is realized as the positive difference of \(S=\{3\}\) and \(T=\{1\}\),
and the \(N=6,g=5\) calculation in the main text realizes the simultaneous
multiplicative collision.

If \(k=|\Delta|\), there are only \(2^k-1\) nonempty subsets of \(\Delta\).
Coverage plus injectivity on \(n\) orders requires \(n\) distinct nonempty
signatures, so
\[
n\le2^k-1,\qquad
k\ge\left\lceil\log_2(n+1)\right\rceil.
\]
Every positive difference is generated by a pair in \(S\times T\), hence
\(|\Delta|\le|S||T|\), proving the second bound.

For the control construction, put \(h=\lceil\sqrt n\rceil\) and
\[
S=\{1+ih:1\le i\le h\},\qquad
T=\{1+j:0\le j<h\}.
\]
Its positive differences are
\[
\{ih-j:1\le i\le h,\ 0\le j<h\}
=\{1,\ldots,h^2\}.
\]
For distinct \(r<s\le n\), candidate \(d=r\) is divisible by \(r\) but not by
\(s\), so it separates. The construction uses
\(|S||T|=h^2=\Theta(n)\) pairs. It proves sufficiency but is exponential when
\(n\) is of magnitude exponential in the binary input length. \(\square\)

\section{Full proof of the conjugate-channel barrier}

\subsection{Proof of BAR-002}\label{proof:BAR-002}

Put \(W_d=a^d+a^{-d}\) in \(\mathbb Z/N\mathbb Z\). Then
\[
W_0=2,\qquad W_1=a+a^{-1}=P(a),
\]
and direct multiplication gives
\[
W_{d+1}=P(a)W_d-W_{d-1}.
\]
The initial values and recurrence uniquely determine the Lucas sequence, so
\[
V_d(P(a),1)=a^d+a^{-d}\pmod N.
\]
Expanding gives
\[
V_d(P(a),1)-2
=a^{-d}(a^d-1)^2\pmod N
\]
and
\[
P(a)^2-4=(a-a^{-1})^2
=a^{-2}(a^2-1)^2\pmod N.
\]

Let \(p^e\Vert N\). Because \(a\) is a unit, the inverse factors have
\(p\)-adic valuation zero. Congruence modulo \(p^e\) therefore gives the
capped identity
\[
\min\{e,\nu_p(V_d(P(a),1)-2)\}
=\min\{e,2\nu_p(a^d-1)\}.
\]
Doubling preserves whether the capped valuation is positive, so the sequence
residues have identical prime support. If \(N\) is square-free, every cap is
one, so the capped valuations, and hence the two raw GCDs, are equal.

Suppose \(G_L(N,P(a),d)\) is proper. Its capped profile is neither all zero nor
full. The undoubled multiplicative profile is positive at exactly the same
primes; wherever the doubled profile is not full, the undoubled profile is
also not full. Thus \(G_M(N,a,d)\) is proper. If
\(G_\Delta(N,P(a))\) is proper, the discriminant identity applies the same
argument to \(a^2-1\), so \(G_M(N,a,2)\) is proper. When \(2\) belongs to the
exponent family, that candidate is present.

The combined family retains every multiplicative candidate. It succeeds
whenever the multiplicative family succeeds, and the preceding implications
show that it cannot succeed on an additional input. Their success domains are
equal. \(\square\)

\section{Full proofs of the nonsplit Lucas results}

\subsection{Proof of LEM-003}\label{proof:LEM-003}

First recall the finite-field structure used in the count. Every finite
multiplicative subgroup \(H\) of a field is cyclic. If \(e\) is its exponent,
then all \(|H|\) elements are roots of \(X^e-1\), so \(|H|\le e\).
Combining elements of maximal prime-power order in the finite abelian group
gives an element of order \(e\), while \(e\le|H|\); hence \(e=|H|\).
Thus \(\mathbb F_q^\times\) and
\(\mathbb F_{q^2}^\times\) are cyclic of orders \(q-1\) and \(q^2-1\).
The norm map \(x\mapsto x^{q+1}\) sends a generator of the latter to an
element of order \(q-1\), so its kernel is cyclic of order \(q+1\).

Let \(\alpha\) be a root of \(X^2-PX+1\). The other root is
\(\alpha^{-1}\), and the recurrence gives
\[
V_d(P,1)=\alpha^d+\alpha^{-d}.
\]
For a nonzero square discriminant, \(\alpha\in\mathbb F_q^\times\); for a
nonsquare discriminant, \(\alpha\) belongs to the norm-one subgroup. In either
group,
\[
\alpha^d+\alpha^{-d}=2
\Longleftrightarrow(\alpha^d-1)^2=0
\Longleftrightarrow\alpha^d=1.
\]
A cyclic group of order \(r\) has \(\gcd(d,r)\) such roots. Away from
\(\alpha=\pm1\), the map
\(\alpha\mapsto P=\alpha+\alpha^{-1}\) has two-element fibers
\(\{\alpha,\alpha^{-1}\}\). The element \(1\) contributes \(P=2\); the
element \(-1\) contributes \(P=-2\) exactly for even \(d\). Pairing all other
roots and counting these shared degenerate parameters once gives
\[
\#\{P:V_d(P,1)=2\}
=\frac{\gcd(d,q-1)+\gcd(d,q+1)}2
\]
and the stated degenerate-root count.

It remains to count nonsplit parameters. The invertible substitution
\(u=P-y,\ v=P+y\) identifies the solutions of
\(y^2=P^2-4\) with the \(q-1\) pairs \(uv=4\). Therefore
\[
\sum_{P\in\mathbb F_q}
\left(1+\left(\frac{P^2-4}{q}\right)\right)=q-1,
\]
so the character sum is \(-1\). There are two zero-discriminant parameters.
If \(n_+\) and \(n_-\) count the nonzero square and nonsquare cases, then
\[
n_++n_-=q-2,\qquad n_+-n_-=-1,
\]
and hence \(n_-=(q-1)/2\). \(\square\)

\subsection{Proof of THM-002}\label{proof:THM-002}

\paragraph{Algorithm.}
On a reached integer \(K\), first run deterministic polynomial-time
primality testing, exact maximal-perfect-power preprocessing, and the even
factor check. For an odd composite non-perfect-power residual, put
\(k=\lceil\log_2K\rceil\), \(M=M_B(k)\), and repeat complete cycles over
\(t=1,\ldots,R(k)\). At every \(t\), sample a fresh exact uniform
\(P\in\{0,\ldots,K-1\}\), put \(d=tM\), and compute
\[
h_\Delta=\gcd(P^2-4,K).
\]
Return it if proper. In every other branch, including \(h_\Delta=K\),
compute
\[
h_L=\gcd(V_d(P,1)-2,K)
\]
and return it if proper. Validate every split and recurse on both children.
Uniform rejection sampling accepts a \(k\)-bit draw with probability greater
than \(1/2\).

\paragraph{One-trial success.}
Fix a witness \((p,q,t)\). Projection of a uniform residue modulo \(K\) to
\(\mathbb F_p\times\mathbb F_q\) is uniform, including when \(K\) has repeated
prime factors or additional factors. At \(p\), LEM-003 supplies
\((p-1)/2\) nonsquare-discriminant parameters. Their roots lie in the
norm-one group of order \(p+1\), so \(p+1\mid d\) gives
\[
V_d(P,1)=2\pmod p.
\]

Since \(d\) is even, the number of nondegenerate parameters modulo \(q\)
that also satisfy \(V_d=2\) is
\[
C_q(d)=
\frac{\gcd(d,q-1)+\gcd(d,q+1)}2-2.
\]
The first GCD is at most \(q-1\). Because \(q+1\nmid d\), the second is a
proper divisor of \(q+1\), hence at most \((q+1)/2\). Thus
\[
C_q(d)\le\frac{3q-9}{4}<\frac{3q}{4}.
\]
Call every other \(q\)-parameter safe. If it is discriminant-degenerate, the
discriminant GCD contains \(q\) but omits the nonsplit prime \(p\), and is
proper. If it is nondegenerate, any discriminant factor is again proper
because it omits \(p\); otherwise the sequence GCD contains \(p\) and omits
\(q\). Every parameter in the event therefore splits \(K\), and its
probability is
\[
\frac{p-1}{2p}\frac{q-C_q(d)}q
\ge\frac13\frac{q-C_q(d)}q>\frac1{12}.
\]

\paragraph{Correctness and termination.}
Only divisors strictly between \(1\) and the current input are returned.
Primality and perfect-power preprocessing preserve factor multiplicities.
At least one \(t\) per cycle is a witness, and its parameter is fresh
conditional on every prior outcome. The probability of surviving \(s\)
cycles is at most \((11/12)^s\), so termination is almost sure and the
expected cycle count is at most 12. The hereditary promise applies at every
reached odd composite non-perfect-power child.

\paragraph{Expected bit complexity.}
Since \(M_B(k)\mid B(k)!\),
\[
\log_2M_B(k)=O(B(k)\log B(k)),
\]
and every exponent has
\(O(B(k)\log B(k)+\log R(k))\) bits. LCM construction, exact sampling,
binary Lucas evaluation, and GCD computation are polynomial for the fixed
polynomial schedule. One cycle has \(R(k)\) trials and the expected number of
cycles is constant. Exact maximal-power preprocessing is polynomial by
bounded integer-root search. Unary perfect-power nodes can be charged to the
following split or leaf, while the binary split tree has \(O(m)\) nodes.
Linearity of expectation gives polynomial expected total time and polynomial
space. \(\square\)

\section{Full proof of the combined-promise density barrier}

\subsection{Proof of BAR-003}\label{proof:BAR-003}

For distinct odd primes \(p,q\), a local \(p-1\) or \(p+1\) asymmetry exists
exactly when some coordinate of \(\sigma_\Delta(p)\) is one and the
corresponding coordinate of \(\sigma_\Delta(q)\) is zero, in either
orientation. This is equivalent to
\(\sigma_\Delta(p)\ne\sigma_\Delta(q)\). A square-free semiprime has no other
composite non-perfect-power divisor, so the local and hereditary-union
conditions agree.

Now let \(S\) contain \(s\ge2\) odd primes, of which \(h\) have nonzero
signature. Every pair wholly outside the hit set has two zero signatures and
is not promised. Therefore the number of promised pairs is at most
\[
\binom{s}{2}-\binom{s-h}{2},
\]
which, after division by \(\binom{s}{2}\), gives
\[
\rho_\Delta(S)\le
\frac{h(2s-h-1)}{s(s-1)}.                         \tag{3}
\]
This need not be equality because two nonzero signatures may coincide.

If \(p\) lies in the hit set, then \(p-1\mid d\) or \(p+1\mid d\) for some
\(d\in\Delta\). For fixed \(d\), every first-type candidate has the form
\(r+1\) and every second-type candidate the form \(r-1\), where \(r\mid d\).
Before imposing primality or membership in \(S\), there are at most
\(2\tau(d)\) candidates. Hence
\[
h\le2\sum_{d\in\Delta}\tau(d).
\]
Pairing divisors below and above \(\sqrt d\) gives
\(\tau(d)\le2\sqrt d\), so, with \(D=\max\Delta\),
\[
h\le4|\Delta|\sqrt D.
\]
The right side of (3) is at most \(2h/(s-1)\); substitution and the trivial
probability bound one prove the stated sparse hit-set bound.

If \(p>D+1\), then both \(p-1\) and \(p+1\) exceed every
\(d\in\Delta\), so neither divides any scheduled exponent and the signature
is zero. Thus a prime set wholly above \(D+1\) has promised-pair density zero.

Finally, for distinct \(p,q\in S_n\),
\[
2^{2n}<pq<2^{2n+1}.
\]
Because an odd semiprime is not a power of two, its binary input length is
\(\lceil\log_2(pq)\rceil=2n+1\), and all pairs use the same schedule.
The strict magnitude hypothesis puts every prime above \(D+1\), giving exact
zero density. The conditional asymptotic statement follows directly from the
sparse hit-set bound. \(\square\)

\section{Full proof of the exponent-encoding divisor barrier}

\subsection{Proof of BAR-004}\label{proof:BAR-004}

Write
\[
d=\prod_i p_i^{a_i},\qquad
\ell=\ell(d),\qquad
T=T_\ell.
\]
Since \(d<2^\ell\), every \(a_i\le\ell\). There are at most \(T\) prime
divisors \(p_i\le T\), and each contributes at most \(\ell+1\) to
\(\tau(d)=\prod_i(a_i+1)\). Their total contribution is at most
\((\ell+1)^T\).

For the remaining primes put \(A=\sum_{p_i>T}a_i\). Each such prime is at
least \(T+1\), so
\[
(T+1)^A
\le\prod_{p_i>T}p_i^{a_i}
\le d<2^\ell.
\]
Thus \(A\le A_\ell\). Since \(a+1\le2^a\) for every nonnegative integer
\(a\), the large-prime contribution is at most
\(2^A\le2^{A_\ell}\). Therefore
\[
\tau(d)\le B(\ell(d))\le Q(\ell(d)).
\]

As \(\ell\to\infty\), \(T_\ell=O(\sqrt\ell)\), while the defining strict
inequality gives
\[
A_\ell<
\frac{\ell}{\log_2(T_\ell+1)}
=O\!\left(\frac{\ell}{\log\ell}\right).
\]
Consequently,
\[
\log_2B(\ell)
=T_\ell\log_2(\ell+1)+A_\ell
=O\!\left(\frac{\ell}{\log\ell}\right).
\]
The function \(x/\log x\) is eventually increasing. Enlarging the constant
for finitely many small arguments and taking the maximum over
\(1\le\ell\le L\) gives
\[
\log_2Q(L)=O\!\left(\frac{L}{\log L}\right).
\]

If an odd prime \(p\) has nonzero signature for \(\Delta(k)\), then some
\(d\in\Delta(k)\) satisfies \(p-1\mid d\) or \(p+1\mid d\). For fixed \(d\),
each candidate prime is one more or one less than a positive divisor of
\(d\), so there are at most \(2\tau(d)\) candidates before primality is
imposed. Hence
\[
|H(k)|
\le2\sum_{d\in\Delta(k)}\tau(d)
\le2E(k)Q(L(k)).
\]

Suppose \(E(k)=k^{O(1)}\) and \(L(k)=o(k\log k)\). To avoid any monotonicity
assumption on \(L\), choose a fixed \(L_0\) beyond the increasing range of
\(x/\log x\). Pointwise for sufficiently large \(k\), if
\(L(k)\le L_0\), then \(Q(L(k))\) is bounded. If
\(L_0<L(k)\le k\), then
\(\log_2Q(L(k))=O(k/\log k)=o(k)\). If \(L(k)>k\), then
\[
\frac{L(k)}{\log L(k)}
\le\frac{L(k)}{\log k}
=o(k).
\]
Thus in all cases \(\log_2Q(L(k))=o(k)\). The polynomial factor \(E(k)\)
is absorbed into the exponent, proving \(|H(k)|\le2^{o(k)}\).

Finally let \(h_k=|H(k)\cap S_k|\). Every promised pair from \(S_k\)
intersects this set by BAR-003, so
\[
\rho_k
\le
\frac{\binom{|S_k|}{2}-\binom{|S_k|-h_k}{2}}
     {\binom{|S_k|}{2}}
\le
\frac{2|H(k)|}{|S_k|-1}.
\]
The assumptions \(|S_k|\ge2^{\alpha k}\) and
\(|H(k)|\le2^{o(k)}\) give
\[
\rho_k\le2^{-\alpha k+o(k)}\longrightarrow0.
\]
No existence or distribution assertion about the stipulated sets \(S_k\)
is used. \(\square\)

\section{Full proof of the multiplication straight-line compression barrier}

\subsection{Proof of BAR-005}\label{proof:BAR-005}

The residue identity follows by induction. At node zero it is the definition.
If it holds for all earlier nodes, then
\[
x_i
=x_{a_i}x_{b_i}
=g^{e_{a_i}}g^{e_{b_i}}
=g^{e_{a_i}+e_{b_i}}
=g^{e_i}\pmod N.
\]

For exponent growth, \(e_0=1=2^0\). Both parents of node \(i\) have indices
at most \(i-1\), so the induction hypothesis gives
\[
e_i=e_{a_i}+e_{b_i}
\le2^{a_i}+2^{b_i}
\le2^{i-1}+2^{i-1}
=2^i.
\]
Thus a \(t\)-multiplication program that realizes formal exponent \(d\) has
\(d\le2^t\), hence
\[
t\ge\lceil\log_2d\rceil\ge\ell(d)-1.
\]
Repeated squaring chooses \((a_i,b_i)=(i-1,i-1)\) and attains
\(e_i=2^i\), proving tightness for powers of two.

Only the \(t+1\) existing nodes are available as outputs. Summing charged
nodes across a fixed number of initial-base programs gives
\[
E(k)\le T(k)+O(1),\qquad L(k)\le T(k)+1.
\]
When \(T(k)=o(k\log k)\), the first quantity is polynomially bounded and the
second is \(o(k\log k)\), so substitution into BAR-004 proves the stated
hit-set and stipulated-population consequences. When \(T(k)=k^{O(1)}\), the
displayed bit-length bound excludes a superpolynomial-bit formal exponent.

Finally,
\[
\log_2\operatorname{tower}(s)
=\log_2(2^{2^s})
=2^s.
\]
The lower bound therefore requires \(2^s\) multiplications, while that many
repeated squarings attain the target. This proves REF-006 inside DEF-010.
\(\square\)

\section{Full proof of the constant-sensitive boundary barrier}

\subsection{Proof of BAR-006}\label{proof:BAR-006}

Write
\[
d=\prod_i p_i^{a_i},\qquad \ell=\ell(d),\qquad T=T_\ell.
\]
There are at most \(T\) prime factors \(p_i\le T\). Since
\(2^{a_i}\le d<2^\ell\), each small-prime contribution \(a_i+1\) is at most
\(\ell+1\), so their product is at most \((\ell+1)^T\).

For the remaining primes put \(A=\sum_{p_i>T}a_i\). Every such prime is at
least \(T+1\), whence
\[
(T+1)^A\le d<2^\ell.
\]
Thus \(A\le A_\ell\). Since \(a+1\le2^a\) for every positive integer \(a\),
the large-prime contribution to \(\tau(d)\) is at most
\(2^A\le2^{A_\ell}\). Therefore
\[
\tau(d)\le C(\ell(d))\le R(\ell(d)).
\tag{11}
\]

As \(\ell\to\infty\),
\[
b_\ell=\log_2\ell+O(1),\qquad
c_\ell=\log_2\log_2\ell+O(1),
\]
and
\[
T_\ell=\frac{\ell}{b_\ell^2c_\ell}(1+o(1)).
\]
It follows that
\[
T_\ell\log_2(\ell+1)
=o\left(\frac{\ell}{\log_2\ell}\right).
\tag{12}
\]
The strict inequality defining \(A_\ell\) gives
\[
A_\ell<\frac{\ell}{\log_2(T_\ell+1)}
\le(1+o(1))\frac{\ell}{\log_2\ell},
\tag{13}
\]
because
\[
\log_2(T_\ell+1)
=\log_2\ell-O(\log_2\log_2\ell).
\]
Equations (12)--(13) prove the one-length leading bound.

It remains to justify the maximum in \(R\). The exact inequalities
\(T_\ell b_\ell\le\ell\) and \(A_\ell<\ell\) give
\[
\log_2C(\ell)\le2\ell.
\tag{14}
\]
For \(\ell\le L/(\log_2L)^2\), equation (14) is
\(o(L/\log_2L)\). For larger \(\ell\),
\[
\log_2\ell=\log_2L-O(\log_2\log_2L),
\]
so the one-length estimate is uniform over this range, and the eventual
monotonicity of \(x/\log_2x\) gives
\[
\log_2R(L)\le(1+o(1))\frac{L}{\log_2L}.
\tag{15}
\]

Every prime in the global hit set is one more or one less than a positive
divisor of some scheduled exponent. Hence
\[
|H(k)|\le2E(k)R(L(k)).
\]
If \(L(k)\le(c+o(1))k\log_2k\), monotonicity lets us substitute the displayed
upper envelope into (15), obtaining
\[
\log_2R(L(k))\le ck+o(k).
\]
For \(c=0\), use the BAR-004 case split instead: bounded \(L(k)\) is trivial;
when \(L(k)\le k\), equation (15) gives \(O(k/\log k)=o(k)\), and when
\(L(k)>k\),
\[
\frac{L(k)}{\log_2L(k)}
\le\frac{L(k)}{\log_2k}
=o(k).
\]
The polynomial \(E(k)\) contributes only \(O(\log k)=o(k)\), proving the
hit-set bound.

Let \(h_k=|H(k)\cap S_k|\). BAR-003 gives
\[
\rho_k
\le
\frac{\binom{|S_k|}{2}-\binom{|S_k|-h_k}{2}}
     {\binom{|S_k|}{2}}
\le\frac{2|H(k)|}{|S_k|-1}.
\]
Under \(|S_k|\ge2^{\alpha k}\) with \(\alpha>c\), this is
\[
\rho_k\le2^{-(\alpha-c)k+o(k)}\longrightarrow0.
\]
BAR-005 supplies \(E(k)\le T(k)+O(1)\) and \(L(k)\le T(k)+1\) for the stated
fixed-base DEF-010 schedule, proving its consequence.

For completeness, let \(P_r=\prod_{j\le r}p_j\). EXT-005 gives
\[
\log_2P_r\le r\log_2p_r=O(r\log r).
\]
Since \(p_j\ge j+1\), the final half of the factors give the matching
\(\Omega(r\log r)\) lower bound. Square-freeness gives
\(\tau(P_r)=2^r\). Scanning its remaining binary digits performs
\(\ell(P_r)-1\) squarings and \(\operatorname{popcount}(P_r)-1\)
multiplications by the base, proving the displayed upper bound; BAR-005 gives
the matching order. Finally, if both \(q-1\) and \(q+1\) divided \(P_r\), then
\(4\mid P_r\), because one neighbor of every odd \(q\) is divisible by four.
This contradicts square-freeness.

For REF-007, the repeated-squaring exponents through \(2^{t(k)}\) have divisor
union exactly \(\{2^j:0\le j\le t(k)\}\). At most two odd-prime candidates
arise from each divisor, so the hit set is polynomial although
\(t(k)=\Theta(k\log k)\). BAR-003's exact pair bound makes the promised
fraction vanish on every stipulated
\(2^{\Omega(k)}\)-size population. \(\square\)

\section{Full proof of the factor-scale primorial barrier}

\subsection{Proof of BAR-007}\label{proof:BAR-007}

Let \(d\mid P_r\) and \(t=\omega(d)\). Since \(P_r\) is square-free, \(d\)
is a product of \(t\) distinct primes. Its least possible value is the product
of the first \(t\) primes. The elementary inequality \(p_j\ge j+1\) gives
\[
d\ge\prod_{j=1}^t p_j\ge(t+1)!.
\tag{16}
\]
Thus every divisor \(d\le X+1\) has \(t\le T(X)\). Choosing its support from
the \(r\) available primes leaves at most \(D(r,X)\) divisors. Every hit
\(q\le X\) uses one of \(d=q-1,q+1\), and each divisor supplies at most two
candidates, proving
\[
|H_k(\beta)|\le2D(r(k),2^{\beta k}).
\tag{17}
\]

For \(t\ge2\), at least \(t/2\) factors of \((t+1)!\) are at least \(t/2\).
Consequently
\[
(t+1)!\ge(t/2)^{t/2}.
\]
Together with \((t+1)!\le2^{\beta k}+1\), this gives
\(T(2^{\beta k})=O(k/\log k)\). Put \(T=T(2^{\beta k})\) and
\(r=r(k)\). If \(r<2T\), then
\[
D(r,2^{\beta k})\le2^r\le2^{2T}=2^{O(k/\log k)}.
\]
Otherwise \(T\le r/2\) and
\[
D(r,2^{\beta k})
\le(T+1)\binom rT
\le(T+1)(er/T)^T.
\tag{18}
\]
For \(r\le Ck\), the function \(t\mapsto t\log(eCk/t)\) is increasing over
the relevant range. Evaluating it at \(O(k/\log k)\) shows
\[
\log_2D(r,2^{\beta k})
=O(k\log\log k/\log k)=o(k).
\tag{19}
\]

For one primorial exponent the nonzero signatures are disjoint: if both
\(q-1\) and \(q+1\) divided \(P_r\), then \(4\mid P_r\), contradicting
square-freeness. If the signature counts on \(S_k\) are \(a,b,z\) for
\((1,0),(0,1),(0,0)\), respectively, the exact promised-pair count is
\[
ab+z(a+b).
\]
Equivalently applying BAR-003 with \(h_k\le|H_k(\beta)|\) gives
\[
\rho_k\le\frac{2h_k}{|S_k|-1}
\le2^{-\alpha k+o(k)}\longrightarrow0.
\tag{20}
\]
A nested primorial list has its hit union contained in its largest member's
hit set.

Finally, an exact DEF-010 construction of \(P_{r(k)}\) using
\(O(k\log k)\) charged nodes has \(\ell(P_{r(k)})=O(k\log k)\) by BAR-005.
Equation (16) with \(d=P_{r(k)}\) then forces \(r(k)=O(k)\), so the theorem
covers every first-primes primorial at that boundary. \(\square\)

\section{Full proof of the general factor-scale boundary barrier}

\subsection{Proof of BAR-008}\label{proof:BAR-008}

Fix \(d=\prod_p p^{a_p}\), \(X\ge3\), and \(Y\ge2\). A divisor
\(e=\prod_p p^{f_p}\) has at most
\[
S_Y(d)=\prod_{p\le Y}(a_p+1)
\]
possible small-prime exponent vectors. If
\(j=\sum_{p>Y}f_p\), then
\[
(Y+1)^j\le e\le X+1,
\]
so \(j\le J_Y(X)\). Label the \(A_Y(d)=\sum_{p>Y}a_p\) available
large-prime occurrences. Map every exponent vector to the subset containing
the first \(f_p\) labels of each prime. This is injective, and therefore
\[
\#\{e:e\mid d,\ e\le X+1\}
\le
S_Y(d)\sum_{j=0}^{\min\{A_Y(d),J_Y(X)\}}\binom{A_Y(d)}j
=F_Y(d,X).
\tag{21}
\]

Now take \(Y=Y_k\), \(X=2^{\beta k}\), and
\(L=\ell(d)\le Ck\log_2k\). Since \(2^{a_p}\le d<2^L\), every
\(a_p+1\le L+1\). There are at most \(Y_k\) small primes, whence
\[
\log_2S_{Y_k}(d)
\le Y_k\log_2(L+1)
=O(k/\log k).
\tag{22}
\]
Also
\[
(Y_k+1)^{A_{Y_k}(d)}\le d<2^L,\qquad
A_{Y_k}(d)=O(k),
\tag{23}
\]
because \(\log_2(Y_k+1)=\Theta(\log k)\). Similarly,
\[
J_{Y_k}(2^{\beta k})
\le\frac{\beta k+1}{\log_2(Y_k+1)}
=O(k/\log k).
\tag{24}
\]

Write \(A=A_{Y_k}(d)\) and \(J=J_{Y_k}(2^{\beta k})\). If \(A<2J\), the
large-prime binomial sum is at most
\[
2^A\le2^{2J}=2^{O(k/\log k)}.
\]
If \(A\ge2J\), monotonicity through \(J\) and the standard elementary
binomial estimate give
\[
\sum_{j=0}^J\binom Aj
\le(J+1)\binom AJ
\le(J+1)(eA/J)^J.
\tag{25}
\]
Indeed, \(\binom AJ\le A^J/J!\), while integrating \(\log x\) gives
\(\log J!\ge J\log J-J+1\) and hence \(J!\ge(J/e)^J\).
For \(A\le C'k\), the function
\(t\mapsto t\log(eC'k/t)\) is increasing over the relevant range. Evaluating
it at \(O(k/\log k)\) shows that the base-two logarithm of (25) is
\[
O(k\log\log k/\log k)=o(k).
\tag{26}
\]
The cases \(A=0\) and \(J=0\) are immediate. Equations (21)--(26) prove the
uniform divisor bound. An exponent \(d=1\) contributes only its single
divisor.

Every hit prime \(q\le2^{\beta k}\) is one more or one less than a divisor
of a scheduled exponent, and the polynomial schedule size contributes only
\(O(\log k)\) to the logarithm. Thus \(|H(k)|=2^{o(k)}\). BAR-003 yields
\[
\rho_k\le\frac{2|H(k)|}{|S_k|-1}
\le2^{-\alpha k+o(k)}\longrightarrow0.
\]
Finally, BAR-005 gives a polynomial number of exposed exponents, each of
length \(O(k\log k)\), for every fixed-base DEF-010 schedule with
\(O(k\log k)\) total charged nodes. This proves the stated transfer.
\(\square\)

\section{Full proof of the addition-subtraction compression barrier}

\subsection{Proof of BAR-009}\label{proof:BAR-009}

The base precheck leaves \(x_0=g\bmod N\) as a unit. Products and inverses of
units remain units, so every requested inverse exists and extended Euclid
computes it in polynomial bit complexity. Inductively,
\[
x_i=x_{a_i}x_{b_i}^{s_i}
=g^{z_{a_i}}g^{s_i z_{b_i}}
=g^{z_{a_i}+s_i z_{b_i}}
=g^{z_i}\pmod N.
\tag{27}
\]

The magnitude bound also follows by induction. The base case is
\(|z_0|=1=2^0\). Since both parents of node \(i\) have index at most \(i-1\),
\[
|z_i|
\le |z_{a_i}|+|z_{b_i}|
\le2^{a_i}+2^{b_i}
\le2^i.
\tag{28}
\]
If \(z_i=d\ne0\), then \(i\ge\lceil\log_2|d|\rceil\). Taking
\(a_i=b_i=i-1\) and \(s_i=+1\) gives \(z_i=2^i\), proving tightness for
positive powers of two.

For \(d>0\),
\[
g^{-d}-1=-g^{-d}(g^d-1)\pmod N.
\tag{29}
\]
The multiplier is a unit modulo \(N\). Multiplication by a unit preserves
every capped prime-power valuation and therefore the exact GCD with \(N\).
When \(d=0\), the residue difference is zero and the GCD is \(N\). Thus signs,
zeros, and duplicates may be removed by retaining precisely the distinct
positive nonzero absolute exponents.

A \(t\)-node table contains \(t\) signs and \(2t\) earlier-parent indices, so
its descriptor has \(O(t\log(t+1))\) bits. Evaluation performs \(t\) modular
multiplications and at most \(t\) extended-GCD inversions, and it exposes at
most \(t+1\) node outputs. For a fixed number of programs with total node
count \(T(k)\), normalization therefore leaves at most \(T(k)+O(1)\)
exponents, each of bit length at most \(T(k)+1\). If
\(T(k)=O(k\log k)\), these are exactly BAR-008's explicit-list and
exponent-length hypotheses. BAR-008 gives the stated \(2^{o(k)}\) hit set,
and BAR-003 gives the stipulated-population bound
\(2^{-\alpha k+o(k)}\). The initial proper-factor GCD exit is a separate
branch and is not used in this exponent-mediated count. \(\square\)

\section{Full proof of the leaf-materialized implicit-batch barrier}

\subsection{Proof of BAR-010}\label{proof:BAR-010}

Choose integer representatives \(a_i=g^{d_i}-1\) and put
\(A=\prod_i a_i\). Since the computed root satisfies \(R\equiv A\pmod N\),
\(\gcd(R,N)=\gcd(A,N)\). For every \(p^e\parallel N\), the exponent of \(p\)
in this GCD is
\[
\min\{e,\nu_p(A)\}
=\min\{e,\sum_i\nu_p(a_i)\},
\tag{30}
\]
with \(\nu_p(0)=+\infty\). Multiplication over the relatively prime exact
prime-power factors of \(N\) proves the displayed formula.

If the root GCD is proper, choose a prime \(p\) dividing it. Then \(p\)
divides some \(a_j\), so \(\gcd(a_j,N)>1\). This leaf GCD cannot be \(N\):
otherwise \(N\mid a_j\), hence \(N\mid A\), contradicting that the root GCD
is proper. Thus the leaf GCD is proper. The converse fails at
\((N,g,\Delta)=(21,2,(2,3))\), where the leaves \(3\) and \(7\) separately
factor \(21\) but their product gives the full root collision.

Begin with \(n\) components, one per materialized leaf. Every binary
multiplication combines two components and decreases the component count by
one. Reaching one root therefore requires exactly \(n-1\) multiplications;
the adjacent-pair tree with odd carries attains the count. DEF-015 also
charges all \(n\) leaf evaluations and materializations, irrespective of
selector syntax, so polynomial total work forces \(n=k^{O(1)}\).

If, for each input length, the complete materialized leaf list is additionally
common and factorization-independent and every exponent has ordinary binary
length \(O(k\log k)\), it meets BAR-008. Its hit-set and stipulated-population
upper bounds follow. The proper-root implication shows that aggregation
cannot add success, while the \(N=21\) witness shows it can mask success. No
claim about a circuit without leaf materialization is used. \(\square\)

\section{Full proof of the explicit-atom product-DAG barrier}

\subsection{Proof of BAR-011}\label{proof:BAR-011}

At atom \(i\), the formal residue identity is the definition because its
multiplicity vector is the \(i\)-th unit vector. If it holds at both parents
of a product gate, then
\[
Y_{\ell_s}Y_{r_s}
\equiv
\prod_i A_i^{\mu_{\ell_s,i}+\mu_{r_s,i}}
=
\prod_i A_i^{\mu_{v_s,i}}
\pmod N.
\tag{31}
\]
Induction proves the identity at every node.

Choose integer lifts of the atom residues. Congruence modulo \(N\) preserves
the GCD with \(N\). For each \(p^e\parallel N\), additivity of valuations on
the used factors gives
\[
\nu_p\left(\prod_i A_i^{\mu_{v,i}}\right)
=
\sum_{i:\mu_{v,i}>0}\mu_{v,i}\nu_p(A_i).
\tag{32}
\]
The restriction to positive multiplicity avoids an unused zero atom and the
undefined expression \(0\cdot(+\infty)\). Capping (32) at \(e\) and
multiplying the relatively prime prime-power components proves the displayed
GCD formula.

Suppose a requested node has proper GCD. A prime dividing it divides at least
one atom with positive multiplicity. That atom GCD exceeds one. It cannot
equal \(N\), because then its positive multiplicity would make the requested
product divisible by \(N\), contradicting properness. Hence a used atom GCD
is proper. Inspecting all stored atoms and reachable nodes visits at most
\(a+t\) nodes and cannot create another source of success.

Every atom has unfolded occurrence count one. At gate one, the sum of its
two parent counts is at most two. Before gate \(s\), every earlier gate has
count at most \(2^{s-1}\), while each atom still has count one, so
\[
W_{v_s}=W_{\ell_s}+W_{r_s}\le2^s.
\tag{33}
\]
Feeding each product node into both inputs of the next gate gives
\(2,4,\ldots,2^s\), proving tightness. Every multiplicity coordinate is at
most \(2^t\) and therefore has \(O(t+1)\) bits, including at \(t=0\). The
full optional table has \((a+t)a\) entries and
\(O(a(a+t)(t+1))\) bits. Residue evaluation uses the
\(a\) explicit atom exponentiations and one multiplication per gate.

For \(N=9,g=4,d_1=1\), the atom is \(3\) with proper GCD \(3\), while its
square is zero modulo \(9\) and has full GCD \(9\). The complementary-support
\(N=21\) collision is identical to BAR-010. Under the separate common
factorization-independent atom-list and \(O(k\log k)\)-bit exponent
hypotheses, the explicit atom list satisfies BAR-008, and the
proper-node-to-atom implication prevents the DAG from enlarging its hit set.
\(\square\)

\section{Full proof of the dyadic telescope barrier}

\subsection{Proof of BAR-012}\label{proof:BAR-012}

For \(t=0\), the empty product and geometric sum both equal one. If the
factorization identity holds at \(t\), multiplication by
\(X^{2^t}+1\) gives
\[
\frac{(X^{2^t}-1)(X^{2^t}+1)}{X-1}
=\frac{X^{2^{t+1}}-1}{X-1}.
\]
Induction proves
\[
\prod_{j=0}^{t-1}(X^{2^j}+1)
=\frac{X^{2^t}-1}{X-1}
=\sum_{i=0}^{2^t-1}X^i.
\]
The recurrence gives \(x_j\equiv g^{2^j}\pmod N\), so
\[
E\equiv DQ\pmod N.
\]
When \(D\) is a unit, multiplying by its unique inverse proves that the
division and product paths agree. A proper denominator GCD is already a
factor, while a full denominator GCD is recorded without attempting an
inverse.

For extraction, use the unreduced integer lifts
\[
\widetilde D=g-1,\qquad
\widetilde F_j=g^{2^j}+1,\qquad
\widetilde E=g^{2^t}-1.
\]
They satisfy
\(\widetilde E=\widetilde D\prod_j\widetilde F_j\) exactly, and reduction
modulo \(N\) preserves each GCD with \(N\). Write
\(N=\prod_{p\mid N}p^{e_p}\), with \(\nu_p(0)=+\infty\). Then
\[
\gcd(Q,N)=
\prod_{p\mid N}
p^{\min\{e_p,\sum_{j<t}\nu_p(\widetilde F_j)\}}.
\]
If this GCD is proper, some lifted factor contributes a positive valuation.
Its individual GCD cannot be \(N\), because then the product GCD would be
full. Hence some \(F_j\) has a proper GCD. Applying the same argument to the
exact lifted numerator identity, with the valuation of \(\widetilde D\)
included, proves that a proper numerator GCD implies a proper denominator or
dyadic-factor GCD. The returned proper value need not be identical: at
\((N,g,t)=(8,1,2)\), the factor GCDs are \(2,2\) while the quotient GCD is
\(4\).

There are exactly \(t\) squarings, \(t\) factor additions, and
\(\max(0,t-1)\) product multiplications. Including denominator and output
work gives \(O((t+1)\poly(\log N))\) bit operations. Degree and monomial
metadata need \(O(t+1)\) bits, while an expanded coefficient list has
\(2^t\) entries. Those entries are terms of one quotient evaluation and do
not provide separate GCD paths. Thus every proper success has a proper
explicit component success, and the compact dyadic telescope is not an
exponential test batch. \(\square\)

\section{Full proof of the arbitrary geometric-sum barrier}

\subsection{Proof of BAR-013}\label{proof:BAR-013}

For \(r\ge1\), splitting the first and second blocks of \(r\) terms gives
\[
S_{2r}(X)=S_r(X)+X^rS_r(X)=S_r(X)(1+P_r(X)),
\qquad P_{2r}=P_r^2.
\]
Appending the last term gives
\[
S_{2r+1}(X)=S_{2r}(X)+X^{2r},
\qquad P_{2r+1}=XP_{2r}.
\]
Starting from \((P_1,S_1)=(X,1)\), induction over the processed binary
prefix proves that DEF-018 constructs
\((P_M,S_M)=(X^M,\sum_{i<M}X^i)\). Adjacent cancellation also gives the
exact identity
\[
(X-1)S_M(X)=X^M-1.
\]
Evaluation at \(g\) modulo \(N\) therefore yields \(E\equiv DQ\pmod N\).

If \(\gcd(D,N)=1\), \(D\) has a unique inverse, division returns \(Q\), and
multiplication by an integer coprime to \(N\) preserves the GCD:
\[
\gcd(E,N)=\gcd(DQ,N)=\gcd(Q,N).
\]
If \(1<\gcd(D,N)<N\), the denominator itself is already a returned factor.
The quotient may differ: \(N=15,g=4,M=2\) gives denominator and quotient
GCDs \(3\) and \(5\), respectively. If \(\gcd(D,N)=N\), then
\(g\equiv1\pmod N\), so all \(M\) sum terms equal one modulo \(N\) and
\[
Q\equiv M\pmod N,\qquad \gcd(Q,N)=\gcd(M,N).
\]
This includes repeated prime powers; \(N=8,g=1,M=4\) gives both GCDs equal
to \(4\). These cases exhaust the denominator status and prove the
extraction trichotomy.

Let \(\ell=\operatorname{bitlength}(M)\). Each of the \(\ell-1\) trailing
bits uses two multiplications and one addition for doubling the prefix; each
trailing one bit adds one multiplication and one addition. The exact counts
are
\[
2(\ell-1)+\operatorname{popcount}(M)-1
\quad\text{and}\quad
(\ell-1)+\operatorname{popcount}(M)-1,
\]
bounded by \(3(\ell-1)\) and \(2(\ell-1)\). For
\(b=\lceil\log_2(|g|+1)\rceil\) and
\(k=\lceil\log_2N\rceil\), reducing the encoded base and performing its GCD
precheck are polynomial in \(b+k\); thereafter the residue
circuit costs \(O(\ell\poly(k))\), and all charged work is polynomial in
\(b+k+\ell\). Degree and monomial-count metadata use \(O(\ell)\) bits, while
the all-one expanded coefficient list has \(M\) entries. Those entries form
one quotient value, not \(M\) independently extracted tests. \(\square\)

\section{Full proof of the nested geometric-quotient barrier}

\subsection{Proof of BAR-014}\label{proof:BAR-014}

Partition the \(AB\) exponents uniquely as \(i=Ar+j\), where
\(0\le r<B\) and \(0\le j<A\). Then
\[
\begin{aligned}
S_{AB}(X)
 &=\sum_{r=0}^{B-1}\sum_{j=0}^{A-1}X^{Ar+j}\\
 &=\left(\sum_{j=0}^{A-1}X^j\right)
   \left(\sum_{r=0}^{B-1}(X^A)^r\right)
 =S_A(X)S_B(X^A).
\end{aligned}
\]
Evaluation gives \(U\equiv LQ\pmod N\). Applying the geometric identity at
the base \(h=g^A\) also gives \(E\equiv CQ\pmod N\).

If \(L\) is a unit, multiplication by \(L\) preserves the GCD with \(N\), so
rational division returns \(Q\) and
\(\gcd(U,N)=\gcd(Q,N)\). If \(L\) has a proper GCD, that value is already a
factor. Equality of returned divisors is false: at
\(N=15,g=2,A=B=2\), the intermediate and quotient GCDs are \(3\) and \(5\),
while the rational numerator GCD is full.

If \(L\equiv0\pmod N\), then
\[
h-1=(g-1)S_A(g)\equiv0\pmod N.
\]
Therefore \(h\equiv1\pmod N\), so \(Q=S_B(h)\equiv B\pmod N\) and
\(\gcd(Q,N)=\gcd(B,N)\). This proves the rational-denominator trichotomy.
Applying BAR-013 at base \(h\) proves the independent composed-denominator
trichotomy for \(C=h-1\).

Let \(\alpha=\operatorname{bitlength}(A)\) and
\(\beta=\operatorname{bitlength}(B)\). The three binary evaluators use
\(O(\alpha)\), \(O(\beta)\), and
\(O(\operatorname{bitlength}(AB))=O(\alpha+\beta)\) modular operations.
Computing \(AB\), reducing the base, performing GCDs and inversions, and
extracting a factor are polynomial in the charged input lengths.

The certificate is described by \((A,B)\). Its quotient monomials have
exponents \(0,A,\ldots,A(B-1)\), so the quotient has degree \(A(B-1)\) and
\(B\) nonzero monomials. Its dense vector has \(A(B-1)+1\) positions. Its
sparse list has \(B\) exponent-coefficient pairs whose exponent fields use
\(O(\alpha+\beta)\) bits, for
\(O(B(\alpha+\beta))\) output bits. The dense \(S_A\) and \(S_{AB}\) outputs
have \(A\) and \(AB\) entries. These charges complete the construction,
evaluation, extraction, and output accounting. \(\square\)

\section{Full proof of the iterated geometric-quotient-chain barrier}

\subsection{Proof of BAR-015}\label{proof:BAR-015}

At stage \(i\), partition every exponent below \(M_i\) uniquely as
\[
t=M_{i-1}q+s,\qquad 0\le q<A_i,\quad 0\le s<M_{i-1}.
\]
Then
\[
\begin{aligned}
S_{M_i}(X)
 &=\sum_{q=0}^{A_i-1}\sum_{s=0}^{M_{i-1}-1}
   X^{M_{i-1}q+s}\\
 &=S_{M_{i-1}}(X)S_{A_i}(X^{M_{i-1}}).
\end{aligned}
\]
Multiplying these linked identities and using \(S_{M_0}=S_1=1\) gives the
chain certificate. Evaluation yields
\[
U_i\equiv L_iQ_i,\qquad L_i=U_{i-1},\qquad
U_r\equiv\prod_iQ_i\pmod N.
\]
Applying \((Y-1)S_{A_i}(Y)=Y^{A_i}-1\) at \(Y=h_i\) also gives
\(E_i\equiv C_iQ_i\pmod N\).

If \(L_i\) is a unit, multiplication by \(L_i\) preserves the GCD with \(N\),
so rational division returns \(Q_i\) and
\(\gcd(U_i,N)=\gcd(Q_i,N)\). If \(L_i\) has a proper GCD, that value already
factors \(N\); equality with the quotient divisor is neither needed nor
generally true. If \(L_i\equiv0\pmod N\), then
\[
C_i=h_i-1\equiv(g-1)S_{M_{i-1}}(g)\equiv0\pmod N.
\]
Thus \(h_i\equiv1\pmod N\), so \(Q_i\equiv A_i\pmod N\) and
\(\gcd(Q_i,N)=\gcd(A_i,N)\). BAR-013 at base \(h_i\) supplies the
independent composed-denominator trichotomy.

For the aggregate, take the integer lifts
\(\widetilde Q_i=S_{A_i}(g^{M_{i-1}})\). Their product is
\(\widetilde U_r\). If \(\gcd(U_r,N)\) is proper, the stage GCDs cannot all
be one, because a product of units is a unit. No stage GCD can be full,
because its quotient is zero modulo \(N\), which would make the product GCD
full. Hence at least one stage quotient GCD is proper.

Let \(L=\sum_i\operatorname{bitlength}(A_i)\). Every prefix product \(M_i\)
has at most \(L\) bits. Three binary evaluators per stage therefore use
\(O(rL)\) modular operations, and \(r\le L\). Prefix multiplication, base
reduction, GCDs, inversions, final multiplication, and extraction are
polynomial in the charged input lengths. Stage \(i\)'s quotient monomials
have exponents
\[
0,M_{i-1},\ldots,(A_i-1)M_{i-1},
\]
so the degree is \(M_i-M_{i-1}\), the sparse count is \(A_i\), and the dense
length is \(M_i-M_{i-1}+1\). Sparse indices use
\(O(\operatorname{bitlength}M_i)\) bits, while a requested dense prefix
\(S_{M_i}\) has \(M_i\) entries. This proves every stated construction,
evaluation, extraction, and output charge. \(\square\)

\section{Full proof of the signed quotient-stage separation}

\subsection{Proof of BAR-016}\label{proof:BAR-016}

For the public chain \((A_1,A_2)=(5,5)\), the prefix exponents are
\(M_0=1,M_1=5,M_2=25\). Modulo \(9\),
\[
S_5(2)=1+2+4+8+7\equiv4,\qquad 2^5\equiv5,
\]
and
\[
S_5(5)=1+5+7+8+4\equiv7.
\]
Thus the stage quotients are \(Q_1=4,Q_2=7\), and the linked prefix
numerators are \(U_1=4\) and \(U_2=4\cdot7\equiv1\). The rational prefixes
are \(L_1=1,L_2=4\). The composed denominators and endpoints are
\[
C_1=2-1=1,\quad E_1=2^5-1\equiv4,\quad
C_2=2^5-1\equiv4,\quad E_2=2^{25}-1\equiv1.
\]
Both public multipliers equal \(5\). Every one of these residues, both
coefficients \(-1,1\), both quotients, and the weighted residues
\(-4\equiv5,7\) has GCD one with \(9\). Their sum is nevertheless
\[
5+7\equiv3\pmod9,\qquad\gcd(3,9)=3.
\]
This proves the claimed component-to-aggregate separation.

The exact formal subtraction is
\[
\begin{aligned}
S_5(X^5)-S_5(X)
 &=(1+X^5+X^{10}+X^{15}+X^{20})
   -(1+X+X^2+X^3+X^4)\\
 &=-X-X^2-X^3-X^4+X^5+X^{10}+X^{15}+X^{20}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Hence its eight nonzero monomials are explicit.

Let \(L=\sum_i\operatorname{bitlength}(A_i)\) and
\(C=\sum_i\operatorname{bitlength}(|c_i|+1)\). Reusing DEF-020 costs
\(O(rL)\) modular operations. Coefficient reduction, \(r\) scalar
multiplications, \(r-1\) additions, requested GCDs, and extraction are
polynomial in the charged input lengths. The double sum defining \(F\)
contains exactly \(\sum_iA_i\) uncollected terms, has degree at most
\(\max_i(M_i-M_{i-1})\), and makes every requested expanded-output cost
explicit. \(\square\)

\section{Full proof of the symmetric signed-difference reduction}

\subsection{Proof of BAR-017}\label{proof:BAR-017}

Write \(n=A-1\). Expanding and pairing the equal constant terms gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
D_A(X)
 &=\sum_{j=1}^{A-1}\left(X^{Aj}-X^j\right)\\
 &=\sum_{j=1}^{A-1}X^j\left(X^{nj}-1\right)\\
 &=X(X^n-1)\sum_{j=1}^{A-1}X^{j-1}S_j(X^n).
\end{aligned}
\]
This proves the stated factorization and the first formula for \(H_A\).
Expanding each \(S_j\) gives exponent pairs
\[
e(j,k)=j-1+nk,\qquad 1\le j\le n,\quad 0\le k<j.
\]
If \(e(j,k)=e(j',k')\), reduction modulo \(n\) gives
\(j=j'\), and then \(k=k'\). Thus all terms are distinct and positive.
There are \(\sum_{j=1}^{n}j=A(A-1)/2\) of them. The maximum, at
\((j,k)=(n,n-1)\), is \(n-1+n(n-1)=A(A-2)\).

Let \(p^e\mid N\) and \(p\nmid g\). Since \(g\) is a \(p\)-adic unit, the
integer factorization gives
\[
v_p(D_A(g))
=v_p(g^{A-1}-1)+v_p(H_A(g)).
\]
Capping both sides at \(e\) yields
\[
\min\{e,v_p(D_A(g))\}=\min\{e,\alpha+\beta\}.
\]
Applying this identity independently to every prime power dividing \(N\)
proves the GCD classification. In the unit-endpoint branch,
\(g(g^{A-1}-1)\) is invertible modulo \(N\), so division of \(D_A(g)\)
recovers \(H_A(g)\) and their GCDs are equal. A proper endpoint is already
a factor. A full endpoint makes \(D_A(g)\equiv0\pmod N\).

For compact evaluation, put \(x=g\bmod N\), \(y=x^n\bmod N\),
\(t_j=x^{j-1}S_j(y)\), and \(z_j=(xy)^j\). The geometric recurrence gives
\[
t_{j+1}=xt_j+z_j,\qquad
z_{j+1}=xy\,z_j,\qquad
h_{j+1}=h_j+xt_j+z_j.
\]
This is exactly the displayed matrix transition with initial state
\((t_1,z_1,h_1)=(1,xy,1)\). Binary powering to exponent \(A-2\) computes
\(h_{A-1}=H_A(g)\) in \(O(\log A)\) modular matrix multiplications. The
expanded output still costs its exact monomial count.

Finally, \(N=9,g=2,A=5\) has endpoint \(2^4-1=15\) and endpoint GCD \(3\).
For \(N=55,g=2,A=3\), the endpoint is \(3\), a unit, while
\(H_3(2)=1+2+8=11\). Hence the difference GCD is \(11\), proving that the
cofactor branch is nonempty. \(\square\)

\section{Full proof of the unequal signed-form reduction}

\subsection{Proof of BAR-018}\label{proof:BAR-018}

Put \(Q_1=S_A(X)\) and \(Q_2=S_B(X^A)\). Since
\[
S_B(Y)-B=(Y-1)\sum_{j=1}^{B-1}S_j(Y),
\]
substitution \(Y=X^A\) and \(X^A-1=(X-1)S_A(X)\) give
\[
Q_2(X)-B=(X-1)Q_1(X)\sum_{j=1}^{B-1}S_j(X^A).
\]
Any common polynomial divisor of \(Q_1,Q_2\) therefore divides \(B\), so it
is constant. At every root \(\zeta\) of the monic degree-\(A-1\) polynomial
\(Q_1\), the displayed identity gives \(Q_2(\zeta)=B\). The root-product definition
of the resultant gives
\(\operatorname{Res}(Q_1,Q_2)=B^{A-1}\). Evaluating that identity at an integer
\(g\) proves
\[
\gcd(Q_1(g),Q_2(g),N)\mid\gcd(B,N).
\]

Let \(L=Q_1(g)\), \(Q=Q_2(g)\), and
\(F=c_1L+c_2Q\pmod N\). If \(L\) is a unit, then
\[
F=L(c_1+c_2QL^{-1})\pmod N,
\]
so the two displayed GCDs agree. A proper \(L\)-GCD is already a factor.
If \(L=0\pmod N\), then
\((g-1)L=g^A-1=0\pmod N\). Hence \(g^A=1\), \(Q=B\), and
\(F=c_2B\pmod N\). This proves the total prefix trichotomy.

Now let \(D=Q_2-Q_1\) and \(h=\gcd(A-1,B-1)\). The constant terms cancel,
so \(X\mid D\). Let \(\zeta\ne1\) satisfy \(\zeta^{A-1}=1\). Then
\(\zeta^A=\zeta\), \(S_A(\zeta)=1\), and
\[
D(\zeta)=S_B(\zeta)-1=\zeta S_{B-1}(\zeta).
\]
This vanishes exactly when \(\zeta^{B-1}=1\). Since
\(D(1)=B-A\ne0\), the common roots with \(X^{A-1}-1\) are precisely the
nontrivial \(h\)-th roots of unity. The endpoint is square-free, so
\[
\gcd_{\mathbb Q[X]}(D,X^{A-1}-1)=S_h(X).
\]
Interchanging \(A,B\) proves the second endpoint-GCD formula. Monic division
then yields \(D=XS_hC_{A,B}\) with \(C_{A,B}\in\mathbb Z[X]\).

For \(p^e\mid N\), the unit base has valuation zero, so
\[
\min\{e,v_p(D(g))\}
=\min\{e,v_p(S_h(g))+v_p(C_{A,B}(g))\}.
\]
Applying this at every prime power proves the unit/proper/full common-factor
trichotomy. In particular, unit division recovers the cofactor residue.

Finally, \(F(0)=c_1+c_2\) and \(F(1)=c_1A+c_2B\), proving the two boundary
factor conditions. They cannot both hold in the unequal nonzero-coefficient
domain. The supports of \(Q_1\) and \(Q_2\) meet only at exponent zero, so
the degree is \(A(B-1)\) and the collected monomial count is \(A+B-2\) when
the constant cancels and \(A+B-1\) otherwise. The cofactor degree follows by
subtracting the degrees of \(X\) and \(S_h\). The two registered witnesses
establish that both residual paths are nonempty. \(\square\)

\section{Full proof of the primitive rational-residue result}

\subsection{Proof of BAR-019}\label{proof:BAR-019}

Let \(d=\gcd(|c_1|,|c_2|)\), \(a_i=c_i/d\), and
\(P=a_1Q_1+a_2Q_2\). Then \(F=dP\). If \(d\) is a unit modulo \(N\),
multiplication by \(d\) preserves the GCD with \(N\). A proper
\(\gcd(d,N)\) is already a factor, while \(N\mid d\) forces
\(F(g)=0\pmod N\). If \(Q_1(g)\) is a unit, ordinary modular inversion gives
\[
F(g)=Q_1(g)\left(c_1+c_2Q_2(g)Q_1(g)^{-1}\right)
\]
and the identical primitive formula. This proves both total trichotomies.

At every root \(\alpha\) of the monic degree-\(A-1\) polynomial \(Q_1\),
the M23 identity gives \(Q_2(\alpha)=B\). The root-product definition
therefore gives
\[
\operatorname{Res}(Q_1,F)=(c_2B)^{A-1}.
\]
At every root of the monic degree-\(A(B-1)\) polynomial \(Q_2\), the
aggregate is \(c_1Q_1\). Reversing the M23 stage resultant introduces sign
\((-1)^{A(A-1)(B-1)}=1\), hence
\[
\operatorname{Res}(Q_2,F)
=c_1^{A(B-1)}\operatorname{Res}(Q_2,Q_1)
=c_1^{A(B-1)}B^{A-1}.
\]

The sharper overlap statements follow without expanding either resultant.
From
\[
F-c_2B
=Q_1\left(c_1+c_2(X-1)\sum_{j=1}^{B-1}S_j(X^A)\right),
\]
every common divisor of \(Q_1(g),F(g),N\) divides \(c_2B\). A common divisor
of \(Q_2(g),F(g),N\) divides \(c_1Q_1(g)\). Multiplying
\[
Q_2-B=(X-1)Q_1\sum_{j=1}^{B-1}S_j(X^A)
\]
by \(c_1\) then shows that it divides \(c_1B\).

A root of \(F\) cannot be a root of \(Q_1\), because there \(F=c_2B\ne0\).
It cannot be a root of \(Q_2\), because stage coprimality gives
\(F=c_1Q_1\ne0\). Thus at a root of unity \(\zeta\ne1\), both denominators
below are nonzero, and multiplying
\[
c_1\frac{\zeta^A-1}{\zeta-1}
+c_2\frac{\zeta^{AB}-1}{\zeta^A-1}=0
\]
by \((\zeta-1)(\zeta^A-1)\) proves the displayed exact root-of-unity
criterion. At \(\zeta=1\), direct evaluation gives
\(c_1A+c_2B=0\). Exact monic division by any requested \(\Phi_n\) is
therefore a valid bounded audit, not a claim that all orders have been
enumerated.

Finally, for \(A=3,B=7,c_1=c_2=1\), direct power summation gives
\(Q_1(i)=i\) and \(Q_2(i)=-i\). Thus the real integer polynomial \(F\)
vanishes at both roots of \(X^2+1\), proving \(\Phi_4\mid F\). At
\(N=55,g=2\), exact evaluation gives stages \(7,8\), aggregate \(15\), and
rational residue \(10\). Their GCD data are respectively \(1,1,5,5\), while
both public overlap bounds are \(\gcd(7,55)=1\). This refutes the simple
boundary/common-step classification and proves the claimed scope
boundary. Compact power descriptors do not materialize their expanded
values; every requested expansion is charged by its actual output length.
\(\square\)

\section{Full proof of the rational root-orbit classification}

\subsection{Proof of THM-003}\label{proof:THM-003}

Let \(R=R_{A,B}(\zeta)\ne0\) be rational and let
\(K=A(B-2)+1\). Since
\[
S_A(\zeta^{-1})=\zeta^{1-A}S_A(\zeta),\qquad
S_B(\zeta^{-A})=\zeta^{-A(B-1)}S_B(\zeta^A),
\]
complex conjugation gives
\[
R_{A,B}(\zeta^{-1})=\zeta^{-K}R.
\]
It fixes rational \(R\), hence \(n\mid K\). In particular
\(\gcd(A,n)=1\). The M24 cleared formula and
\(\zeta^{AB}=\zeta^{2A-1}\) then give
\[
R+1=\zeta\left(\frac{\zeta^{A-1}-1}{\zeta^A-1}\right)^2. \tag{20}
\]

Choose \(bA\equiv1\pmod n\), set \(k\equiv b-1\pmod n\), and apply the
automorphism \(\zeta\mapsto\zeta^b\) to (20). Rationality fixes the left side,
while elementary simplification of the right side gives
\[
T:=R+1
=\frac{(1-\zeta^k)(1-\zeta^{-k})}
        {(1-\zeta)(1-\zeta^{-1})}
=u\overline u,\qquad u=\frac{1-\zeta^k}{1-\zeta}. \tag{21}
\]
The geometric sum \(u\) is an algebraic integer. Thus rational \(T\) is an
integer, and in the distinguished complex embedding
\[
T=\frac{\sin^2(k\pi/n)}{\sin^2(\pi/n)}\ge0. \tag{22}
\]
If \(T=0\), then \(k=0\), so \(A\equiv1\pmod n\); the phase congruence then
gives \(B\equiv1\pmod n\). This is the common-step family with \(R=-1\).
The case \(T=1\) would give \(R=0\), contrary to the nonzero second stage.
At \(n=2\), being outside both stage zero sets forces \(A,B\) odd and is
already common-step. We may therefore assume \(n>2\) and \(T\ge2\).

Put \(d=\gcd(n,k)\) and \(m=n/d>1\), the order of \(\zeta^k\).
The conjugates of \(\zeta^k\) run through every primitive \(m\)-th root
\(\varphi(n)/\varphi(m)\) times. Taking the absolute field norm in (21)
therefore yields
\[
T^{\varphi(n)/2}
=\frac{\Phi_m(1)^{\varphi(n)/\varphi(m)}}{\Phi_n(1)}. \tag{23}
\]
For \(s>1\), \(\Phi_s(1)=p\) when \(s=p^a\), and equals one when \(s\)
has at least two prime divisors. The prime-power statement follows from the
quotient of \(X^{p^a}-1\) by \(X^{p^{a-1}}-1\). For
\(s=p^a r\), \(r>1\), \(p\nmid r\), taking \(X\to1\) in
\[
\Phi_{p^a r}(X)=
\frac{\Phi_r(X^{p^a})}{\Phi_r(X^{p^{a-1}})}
\]
proves the second statement.

Suppose first that \(n=p^a\). Then \(m=p^c\), and (23) gives
\[
v_p(T)=\frac{2}{\varphi(m)}-\frac{2}{\varphi(n)}.
\]
If \(m=n\), then \(T=1\). If \(m<n\), a positive integral valuation occurs
only when \(p=2,a=2,c=1\). Thus
\((n,m,T)=(4,2,2)\). Here \(k=2,b=3\), so
\(A\equiv3\pmod4\), and the phase congruence forces
\(B\equiv3\pmod4\). Hence \(R=1\).

Now suppose \(n\) is not a prime power. If \(m\) is not a prime power,
(23) again gives \(T=1\). Otherwise \(m=p^c\) and
\[
T=p^{2/\varphi(m)}.
\]
Integrality forces \(\varphi(m)\mid2\), leaving
\((m,T)=(2,4),(3,3),(4,2)\). Equation (22) resolves them exactly.
For \(m=2\), the numerator is one and \(T=4\) forces
\(\sin^2(\pi/n)=1/4\), hence \(n=6\). For \(m=3\), the numerator is
\(3/4\), and \(T=3\) again forces \(n=6\). For \(m=4\), the numerator is
\(1/2\), and \(T=2\) would force \(n=6\), contradicting \(4\mid n\).
At \(n=6,m=2\), \(k=3\) makes \(b=4\) a nonunit, impossible. At
\(n=6,m=3\), only \(k=4\) makes \(b=k+1=5\) a unit. Thus
\(A\equiv5\pmod6\), the phase congruence gives
\(B\equiv3\pmod6\), and \(R=T-1=2\).

Conversely, in the common-step family both stage values equal one. Direct
substitution in (21) gives \(T=2\) in the stated order-four family and
\(T=3\) in the stated order-six family, proving ratios \(1\) and \(2\).
The primitive coefficient pairs follow from \(c_1/c_2=R\).

Requested-order recognition is a constant number of comparisons, GCDs, and
modular reductions in the binary inputs. For fixed \(A,B\), the full compact
descriptor is all divisors greater than one of
\(\gcd(A-1,B-1)\), plus the enabled orders four and six. Factoring that GCD,
enumerating divisors, or expanding cyclotomic polynomials is charged
separately. Finally, \((A,B,n)=(2,4,5)\) satisfies the phase congruence but
(22) gives \(R=(1+\sqrt5)/2\), refuting its sufficiency. \(\square\)

\section{Full proof of exceptional cyclotomic extraction}

\subsection{Proof of BAR-020}\label{proof:BAR-020}

For \(A=4a+3\), the block identity
\[
S_A(x)=\Phi_4(x)\bigl((1+x)S_a(x^4)+x^{4a}\bigr)+x^{A-2}
\]
is a direct expansion. Apply the same identity to \(S_B(x^A)\). Since
\(A\) is odd,
\[
\frac{\Phi_4(x^A)}{\Phi_4(x)}
=\frac{x^{2A}+1}{x^2+1}=S_A(-x^2).
\]
The two remainders have exponents \(r=A-2\equiv1\pmod4\) and
\(s=A(B-2)\equiv3\pmod4\). Hence \(k=(s-r)/2\) is odd and
\[
\frac{x^r+x^s}{x^2+1}=x^rS_k(-x^2).
\]
Adding the three quotient terms proves the displayed formula for \(C_4\).

For the second family,
\[
S_6=\Phi_6H,\qquad x^6-1=\Phi_6J.
\]
Thus, for \(A=6a+5\), \(B=6b+3\), and \(y=x^A\),
\[
S_A(x)=\Phi_6(x)H(x)S_{a+1}(x^6)-x^A
\]
and
\[
S_B(y)=\Phi_6(y)\bigl(H(y)S_b(y^6)+y^{6b}\bigr)+2y^{6b+1}.
\]
It remains to divide \(\Phi_6(x^A)\) by \(\Phi_6(x)\). With ascending
coefficients, division by \(x^2-x+1\) obeys
\(q_j=p_j+q_{j-1}-q_{j-2}\). Its impulse response is
\[
h=(1,1,0,-1,-1,0).
\]
The coefficient \(-1\) at degree \(A\equiv5\pmod6\) changes the next
\(A-1\) coefficients to
\[
k=(-1,0,1,1,0,-1).
\]
The coefficient at degree \(2A\) makes the next two recurrence values zero,
so
\[
D_6(A;x)=\sum_{j=0}^{A-1}h_{j\bmod6}x^j+
x^A\sum_{j=0}^{A-2}k_{j\bmod6}x^j
\]
is the exact finite quotient. Substituting the two stage identities and
using
\[
x^{6Ab}-1=(x^6-1)S_{Ab}(x^6)
\]
proves the displayed \(C_6\) formula.

Each period-six sum is one fixed block polynomial times
\(S_{\lfloor L/6\rfloor}(x^6)\), plus at most five tail terms. Binary
geometric-sum composition therefore evaluates every term in
\(O(\log A+\log B)\) modular operations. Product exponents such as \(Ab\)
have only \(O(\log A+\log B)\) bits. No inverse and no unknown factor is
used.

Both \(F_i\) are monic of degree \(A(B-1)\); division by a monic quadratic
gives a monic cofactor of degree \(A(B-1)-2\). Dense output therefore has
\(A(B-1)-1\) coefficients and is charged separately. Finally,
\(F_i(g)=\Phi_i(g)C_i(g)\) is an integer product, so valuations add and
remain correct after capping at every \(p^e\parallel N\). Proper computed
GCDs extract factors. Unit multiplication preserves GCD with \(N\); a full
cyclotomic collision makes the aggregate full but leaves the independently
computed cofactor available. These are all branches. \(\square\)

\clearpage
\section{Full proof of the fixed exceptional-cofactor schedule barrier}

\subsection{Proof of BAR-021}\label{proof:BAR-021}

For \(N=\prod p^{e_p}\), unique factorization gives
\[
\gcd(C_i(g),N)=
\prod_{p\mid N}p^{\min(v_p(C_i(g)),e_p)}.
\]
The product is proper exactly when at least one capped exponent is positive
and at least one is not full. Monicity of \(C_i\) makes its reduction modulo
every prime a nonzero degree-\(d\) polynomial, proving the stated unit-root
upper bound.

At every nontrivial \(A\)-th root, \(S_B(X^A)=B\), so
\[
|\operatorname{Res}(S_A,F_i)|=B^{A-1}.
\]
The second-stage resultant additionally contributes the first coefficient to
the power \(\deg S_B(X^A)=A(B-1)\). Evaluation at primitive fourth and sixth
roots gives
\[
\begin{gathered}
|\operatorname{Res}(S_A,\Phi_4)|
=|\operatorname{Res}(S_B(X^A),\Phi_4)|
=|\operatorname{Res}(S_A,\Phi_6)|=1,\\
|\operatorname{Res}(S_B(X^A),\Phi_6)|=4,
\end{gathered}
\]
the last because \(S_B(\zeta_6^A)=2\zeta_6^{-1}\). Multiplicativity of the
resultant with \(F_i=\Phi_iC_i\) proves all four stage/cofactor formulas.

Reducing the exact M26 quotient identities by \(X^2+1\) gives
\[
C_4\equiv
\frac{A(B+2)+1}{4}+
\frac{A(B-2)+1}{4}X.
\]
Reducing by \(X^2-X+1\) gives
\[
C_6\equiv
-\frac{2(A(B-2)+1)}{3}+
\frac{A(B+4)+4}{3}X.
\]
The family congruences make the coefficients integral. The norm of \(u+vX\)
is \(u^2+v^2\) in the first quadratic and \(u^2+uv+v^2\) in the second.
Both forms are positive here, proving the displayed resultants and excluding
a repeated cyclotomic factor over \(\mathbb Q\).

Now fix a finite public tuple set. At every \(g\ge2\), the base, both
geometric stages, both cyclotomic polynomials, and both exceptional
numerators are positive. Since \(F_i=\Phi_iC_i\), each cofactor value is also
a positive integer. Multiply these values with all public stage bounds and
\(R_i\). Only finitely many primes divide the resulting nonzero integer.
Choose distinct primes \(p,q\) outside that support. For \(N=pq\), every
charged value is coprime to \(N\), so every charged GCD is one. Infinitely
many primes remain outside a finite support, hence infinitely many such
semiprimes exist.

The closed \(u_i,v_i,R_i\) have \(O(\log A+\log B)\) bits, while the stage
resultants are retained as compact base/exponent descriptors. Local compact
evaluation is polynomial in the encoded inputs, but enumerating all residues
modulo \(p\) costs \(p-1\) trials. The proof chose \(N\) after one fixed
finite schedule; changing to a length-indexed or adaptive schedule changes
the quantifiers and is outside the theorem. \(\square\)

\subsection{Proof of BAR-022}\label{proof:BAR-022}

Fix \(m\) with \(s_m=|\mathcal P_m|\ge2\). From the definition of
\(\mathcal P_m\), every \(p,q\in\mathcal P_m\) satisfy
\[
 2^{(m-1)/2}\le p,q<2^{m/2}.
\]
Thus every distinct pair obeys \(2^{m-1}\le pq<2^m\). Since \(pq\) is odd,
it is not a power of two, and its repository input length is exactly \(m\).
Also \(p\ge2^{b_m}\) for
\(b_m=\lfloor(m-1)/2\rfloor\).

Let \(H_m\) be the population primes dividing at least one nonzero exact lift
\(z_{m,j}\). If distinct \(p,q\notin H_m\), then
\[
 \gcd(z_{m,j},pq)=1
\]
for every \(j\). The pairs with both endpoints outside \(H_m\) are exactly
the \(\binom{s_m-h_m}{2}\) forced misses. Every other pair has at least one
endpoint in \(H_m\), so at most
\(\binom{s_m}{2}-\binom{s_m-h_m}{2}\) pairs can be touched. This is only an
upper bound: a value divisible by both endpoints gives the full collision
\(pq\), not a proper factor.

Put \(D_m=\prod_j|z_{m,j}|\). The square-free product of the distinct primes
in \(H_m\) divides \(D_m\), hence
\[
 2^{b_mh_m}
 \le\prod_{p\in H_m}p
 \le D_m.
\]
Taking base-two logarithms and using
\(\log_2|z_{m,j}|\le\operatorname{bitlength}(|z_{m,j}|)\) proves
\[
 b_mh_m\le W_m.
\]
If \(h_m\le s_m-2\), two population primes remain outside the support and
their product is a miss. Universal population coverage therefore requires
\(h_m\ge s_m-1\), and substitution gives
\(W_m\ge b_m(s_m-1)\).

It remains to verify why this materialized bound does not apply to the
compact ledger. For \(t\ge2\), set \(A=3\), \(B=2^t+3\), and \(g=2\).
Both \(A\) and \(B\) are \(3\bmod4\), so the order-four exceptional identity
is valid and gives
\[
 5C_4(2)=S_3(2)+S_B(8)=7+\frac{8^B-1}{7}.
\]
The parameter encodings and binary recurrence counts have \(O(t)\) bits,
so the M26 formula evaluates the residue modulo \(N\) in polynomial time in
\(t+\operatorname{bitlength}(N)\). On exact integers,
\[
 C_4(2)>\frac{8^{B-1}}5>2^{3B-6},
\]
which implies
\[
 \operatorname{bitlength}(C_4(2))\ge3B-5=3\cdot2^t+4.
\]
This refutes the compact-to-materialized bit-budget implication. It says
nothing about how many distinct primes divide the cofactor. All prime
divisors used above are analytical objects, not constructor inputs. No
asymptotic lower bound for \(s_m\), promise recognizer, adaptive schedule
bound, or general factoring lower bound has been used. \(\square\)

\subsection{Proof of BAR-023}\label{proof:BAR-023}

Set \(B_m=2^m+3\) and \(E_m=3B_m-4=3\cdot2^m+5\). The exceptional
identity at \(g=2\) gives
\[
5C_m=7+\frac{8^{B_m}-1}{7},
\]
and therefore
\[
35C_m=8^{B_m}+48=16(2^{E_m}+3).
\]
For \(m\ge2\), \(E_m\equiv1\pmod4\) and \(E_m\equiv2\pmod3\).
Thus \(2^{E_m}+3\) is divisible by both 5 and 7. It is odd, so
\(v_2(C_m)=4\). Since \(E_m\) is odd,
\(2^{E_m}+3\equiv2\pmod3\), proving \(3\nmid C_m\).

A factor 5 remains after division by 35 exactly when
\(2^{E_m}\equiv-3\pmod{25}\). The order of 2 modulo 25 is 20 and
\(2^{17}\equiv-3\pmod{25}\), so this is equivalent to
\(E_m\equiv17\pmod{20}\), then to \(2^m\equiv4\pmod{20}\), and finally to
\(m\equiv2\pmod4\). For 7, use
\[
16(2^{E_m}+3)=8^{B_m}+48
\equiv1+7B_m+48\equiv7B_m\pmod{49}.
\]
A second factor of 7 is present exactly when \(7\mid B_m\), or
\(2^m\equiv4\pmod7\), which is \(m\equiv2\pmod3\). For every \(p>7\),
16 and 35 are units, so the displayed closed form proves
\[
p\mid C_m\Longleftrightarrow2^{E_m}\equiv-3\pmod p.
\]

Both consecutive cofactors contain exactly \(2^4\). Neither contains 3, and
the congruence classes for 5 and 7 are not consecutive. If \(p>7\) divided
both, then \(E_{m+1}=2E_m-5\) would give
\[
-3\equiv2^{E_{m+1}}
=2^{2E_m-5}\equiv\frac{(-3)^2}{32}=\frac9{32}\pmod p.
\]
Hence \(p\mid105\), a contradiction. Therefore
\(\gcd(C_m,C_{m+1})=16\).

Finally, for distinct odd \(p,q\), the GCD \(\gcd(C_m,pq)\) is proper
exactly when one factor divides \(C_m\) and the other does not. A support of
size \(h\) inside \(s\) primes therefore gives \(h(s-h)\) cross-cut proper
outcomes, \(\binom h2\) hit--hit full collisions, and
\(\binom{s-h}{2}\) miss--miss units. The product \(h(s-h)\) is at most
\(\lfloor s^2/4\rfloor\), which is strictly below \(\binom{s}{2}\) for
\(s\ge3\). The compact evaluator uses \(O(m)\)-bit counts and polynomial
modular work; none of the analytical support primes are constructor inputs.
No multi-candidate, density, adaptive-schedule, general factoring, or
general-circuit conclusion is used. \(\square\)

\subsection{Proof of BAR-024}\label{proof:BAR-024}

Fix \(m\), a population \(\mathcal P_m\) of \(s\ge2\) distinct odd primes,
and nonzero exact candidate lifts \(z_1,\ldots,z_r\). For
\(N=pq\) with distinct population primes,
\[
\gcd(z_j,N)
=p^{\mathbf1_{p\mid z_j}}q^{\mathbf1_{q\mid z_j}}.
\]
Equal zero bits give the unit GCD, equal one bits give the full collision,
and unequal bits give one proper prime factor. Consequently some candidate
succeeds exactly when \(\sigma(p)\ne\sigma(q)\).

For each \(u\in\{0,1\}^r\), let
\(n_u=|\{p:\sigma(p)=u\}|\). Precisely the pairs within a common signature
bucket fail, so
\[
F=\sum_u\binom{n_u}{2},\qquad
S=\binom s2-F.
\]
Thus all pairs succeed if and only if every bucket has size at most one,
which is exactly injectivity. Since there are \(2^r\) buckets,
\(s\le2^r\), proving \(r\ge\lceil\log_2s\rceil\). If zero signatures are
forbidden, only \(2^r-1\) buckets remain and
\(r\ge\lceil\log_2(s+1)\rceil\).

It remains to minimize \(F\). If two occupied buckets have sizes
\(x\ge y+2\), moving one prime from the larger bucket to the smaller changes
their contribution by
\[
\binom{x-1}{2}+\binom{y+1}{2}
-\binom x2-\binom y2=y-x+1<0.
\]
Every minimizing assignment therefore has bucket sizes differing by at most
one. With \(t=2^r\) and \(s=bt+a\), exactly \(a\) buckets have size \(b+1\)
and \(t-a\) have size \(b\), giving
\[
F_{\min}=(t-a)\binom b2+a\binom{b+1}{2}.
\]
Removing the zero bucket gives the stated nonzero-signature variant.

Distinct labels can be realized abstractly by taking coordinate \(j\) to be
the product of population primes whose label has bit \(j\). That tightness
construction enumerates the unknown primes, uses their values, and
materializes their products; it is not a compact factorization-independent
schedule. Conversely, candidates \((15,7)\) on \(\{3,5,7\}\) give signatures
\((1,1,2)\), so coverage and the candidate count do not imply injectivity.
The canonical-prefix observations in EXP-0029 are finite and are not used in
this proof. No recognizer, density theorem, general factoring conclusion, or
general circuit lower bound follows. \(\square\)

\subsection{Proof of THM-004 and BAR-025}
\label{proof:THM-004-BAR-025}

For \(m\ge9\), construct \(\mathcal T_m\) exactly as in DEF-031. There are at
most two families, \((m-1)^2\) ordered parameter pairs, and \(m-1\) bases, so
\[
|\mathcal T_m|\le2(m-1)^3.
\]
Every parameter and base has \(O(\log m)\) bits. BAR-020 evaluates each
cofactor in \(O(\log A+\log B)\) modular steps, and BAR-021 computes each
public resultant with \(O(\log m)\)-bit arithmetic. Thus construction and
all charged evaluations, GCDs, outputs, and extraction have polynomial bit
cost in the \(m\)-bit input.

If \(p\in\mathcal P_m\), then \(p\ge2^{(m-1)/2}>m\); the final inequality
holds at \(m=9\) and persists by induction. Hence every scheduled base and
public parameter is smaller than the odd population prime. All unit-only
exceptional evaluations are therefore defined on the promised inputs.

For any charged integer \(z\), write \(h_z(p)=\mathbf1_{p\mid z}\).
Constant columns distinguish no pair, and duplicate columns have the same
equality relation as one copy. Aggregate support equals the OR of the
cyclotomic and cofactor columns, while an overlap support is an AND of its
constituent columns. Therefore equality of normalized signatures is
equivalent to equality across all charged exits. BAR-024 now proves that the
selector succeeds on \(pq\) exactly when the normalized signatures differ.

The registered certificate enumerates every prime in each complete
\(\mathcal P_m\) for \(9\le m\le15\). Its population sizes are
\[
2,3,3,4,6,7,11,
\]
and the checked restricted signatures are pairwise distinct using
\[
1,2,2,3,4,6,10
\]
public columns, respectively. The dense verifier expands every selected
cofactor, recomputes the exact column residues, and checks all 104 pairs.
This proves THM-004 by complete finite exhaustion.

At \(m=16\), direct dense evaluation of all eight primitive exits for every
one of the 270 descriptors gives identical masks for \(191,227,233\).
Derived aggregate and overlap coordinates are then identical as well.
BAR-024 makes each of their three pair products a failure, proving BAR-025
and refuting REF-027. The certificate contains no statement about another
selector or an unbounded range. \(\square\)

\subsection{Proof of THM-005 and BAR-026}
\label{proof:THM-005-BAR-026}

Fix \(m\ge9\). For public caps \(m\le L\le L'\), every valid descriptor
through \(L\) is also present through \(L'\). The raw signature at \(L\) is
therefore a coordinate projection of the raw signature at \(L'\), so an
already separated pair cannot merge. At either fixed cap, deletion of
constant columns and identification of duplicate columns preserve equality
of prime signatures by the DEF-031 normalization proof. This establishes
pair-separation monotonicity without asserting monotonicity of normalized
column counts.

EXP-0031 enumerates every integer cap from \(m\) to the first injective cap
for \(16\le m\le20\). At the reported predecessor, an independent dense
evaluator checks identical branch-total masks for every descriptor on each
reported collision bucket. The collision and raw inclusion prove failure at
every smaller cap. At the threshold, the registered public coordinate
sublist has pairwise distinct packed signatures on the complete population.
Its sizes are \(10,16,20,26,40\), and all 1,930 prime pairs were checked
independently. Thus the exact threshold sequence is
\[
19,19,27,27,31.
\]

THM-004 already gives injectivity at cap \(m\) for \(9\le m\le15\).
For \(16\le m\le20\), \(m+11\) is at least the displayed threshold.
Monotonicity and BAR-024 prove THM-005. Offset 10 fails at
\(m=20,L=30\) on \(\{809,827\}\), so 11 is the smallest common integer
offset on the finite range.

For \(L_c(m)=\lceil cm\rceil\), meeting threshold \(L_m^\star\) is
equivalent to \(c>(L_m^\star-1)/m\). The five right-hand sides are
\[
\frac{18}{16},\quad\frac{18}{17},\quad\frac{26}{18},\quad
\frac{26}{19},\quad\frac{30}{20}.
\]
Their maximum is \(3/2\), proving the strict coefficient condition in
BAR-026. The endpoint fails on the registered length-20 collision, while
any \(c>3/2\) exceeds all five ratios. This is a complete finite proof, not
an asymptotic claim. \(\square\)

\subsection{Proof of THM-006 and BAR-027}
\label{proof:THM-006-BAR-027}

At \(m=21\), direct evaluation of the two fixed M32 formulas gives cap 32.
The registered complete population contains 57 primes. Independent dense
evaluation of all eight branch-total exits for all 2,511 cap-32 descriptors
gives identical masks on \(1031,1231,1319,1433\). BAR-024 therefore makes
all six pair products failures, proving the recurrence part of BAR-027 and
refuting REF-029.

At cap 33, the registered 53-coordinate sublist gives pairwise distinct
packed signatures on all 57 primes. The dense verifier checks all 1,596
pairs. Hence cap 33 is injective. Since cap 32 collides and every smaller
selector is its subset, \(L_{21}^\star=33\) exactly.

The M32 table has \(t_{20}^\star=11\), so the new offset 12 gives
\(t_{21}^\star=12\). Raw monotonicity transfers every earlier construction,
proving THM-006. For multiplicative caps, the new strict threshold is
\[
\frac{L_{21}^\star-1}{21}=\frac{32}{21}>\frac32,
\]
so \(c>32/21\) is necessary and sufficient on the complete finite range.
The coefficient \(151/100\) lies below it and fails, while \(153/100\) lies
above it and succeeds. No later length or asymptotic rate follows.
\(\square\)

M33 gives \(t_{21}^\star=12\), so the new offset 17 gives

\subsection{Proof of THM-014 and BAR-035}
\label{proof:THM-014-BAR-035}

At \(m=29\), the fixed M40 formulas give distinct caps 105 and 108.
Losslessly packing the eight primitive exits of every descriptor gives 685
distinct complete raw signatures at both caps. Thus both pre-registered
schedules are injective on the new complete population.

For minimality, the adjacent raw profiles give 684 signatures at cap 102
and 685 at cap 103. The only failed cap-102 pair is
\(\{18979,21031\}\). Raw selector inclusion makes every lower cap a subset
of the failed cap-102 selector. The complete cap-103 normalized profile has
1,555 columns and also separates all 685 primes.

Among the 5,989 descriptors added at cap 103, exhaustive independent
evaluation of all 47,912 primitive coordinates finds exactly one that is
nonconstant on the predecessor pair:
\[
\texttt{phi4:87:95:103:cofactor}=(0,1).
\]
One cap-102 representative for each old normalized support mask gives 1,527
columns. Appending the displayed coordinate produces pairwise distinct
packed signatures on all 685 primes, and the independent verifier checks all
234,270 pairs. The cap-102 collision proves that at least one new coordinate
is necessary; the displayed coordinate proves one is sufficient. Hence
\(L_{29}^{\star}=103\) exactly.

The new local additive offset is \(103-29=74\), below the controlling M40
value \(104-28=76\). Therefore \(m+76\) remains the smallest common offset
through length 29, proving THM-014. The new multiplicative endpoint is
\[
\frac{L_{29}^{\star}-1}{29}=\frac{102}{29}
 < \frac{103}{28},
\]
so the exact finite coefficient condition also remains \(c>103/28\).
Finally, \(L_{29}^{\star}=103<104=L_{28}^{\star}\) refutes REF-037. Since
the two thresholds concern different prime populations, no asymptotic trend
follows. \(\square\)

\]
at caps 112 through 123. Cap 122 has the sole collision triple

\[
\texttt{phi4:123:59:87:cofactor}=(0,0,1),\qquad
\texttt{phi4:79:123:54:cofactor}=(1,0,0).
\]
Appending both gives pairwise distinct packed signatures on all 927 primes,
and the independent verifier checks all 429,201 pairs. One bit has only two
values and cannot distinguish three primes; the displayed two bits do.
Hence the minimum incremental repair size is two.

The new local additive offset is \(123-30=93\), larger than every prior
finite row, so \(m+93\) is the smallest common offset through length 30,

\[
\frac{L_{30}^{\star}-1}{30}=\frac{122}{30}=\frac{61}{15}
 > \frac{103}{28}.
\]
Thus the exact finite coefficient condition becomes \(c>61/15\). The
endpoint fails at cap 122, while \(49/12>61/15\) gives cap 123. No later
length or asymptotic rate follows. \(\square\)

\subsection{Proof of BAR-041}
\label{proof:BAR-041}

Fix a valid descriptor with \(2\le A,B,g\le L\), and put
\(b=\operatorname{bitlength}(L)\). The positive geometric sums satisfy
\[
S_A(g)<g^A<2^{Lb},\qquad
S_B(g^A)<g^{AB}<2^{L^2b}.
\]
The direct cyclotomic is at most \(g^2+1<2^{2b+1}\). In both exceptional
families, inspection of the exact linear remainder formulas shows that each
remainder coefficient has magnitude at most \(L^2\); their quadratic norm
is positive and at most \(3L^4<2^{4b+2}\). Finally,
\[
0<C_i(g)
\le\alpha_iS_A(g)+S_B(g^A)
<2g^{AB}<2^{L^2b+1}.
\]
Together with the base and two public-bound rows, the eight bit lengths sum
to at most \(2L^2b+Lb+9b+5\). Multiplying by the descriptor upper bound
\(2(L-1)^3\) proves (M47.1).

Let \(H_{m,L}\) be the population primes dividing at least one charged exact
integer. Each such prime is at least
\(2^{\lfloor(m-1)/2\rfloor}\), and their square-free product divides the
product of the exact integers. Taking base-two logarithms gives
\[
\left\lfloor\frac{m-1}{2}\right\rfloor |H_{m,L}|
\le W(m,L).
\]

For \(x=2^{m/2}\), neither endpoint \(x,x/\sqrt2\) is prime: one is a power
of two and the other is nonintegral. Hence
\[
|\mathcal P_m|=\pi(x)-\pi(x/\sqrt2).
\]
The inspected Rosser--Schoenfeld bounds apply for \(m\ge10\). Moreover,
\[
128\cdot50000^2-81\cdot62753^2
=1{,}026{,}940{,}271>0,
\]
so \(1.25506/\sqrt2<8/9\). Since \(m/(m-1)\le10/9\),
\[
|\mathcal P_m|
>\frac{x}{\log x}
\left(1-\frac{1.25506}{\sqrt2}\frac{m}{m-1}\right)
>\frac{x}{81\log x},
\]
which is (M47.2).

If \(L(m)\le Cm^d\) eventually, (M47.1) and the support inequality bound
\(|H_{m,L(m)}|\) by a polynomial in \(m\), whereas (M47.2) is exponential up
to a polynomial factor. Eventually two population primes lie outside the
union support. The base branch and every continuation primitive are zero on
both signatures; derived coordinates are Boolean functions of those bits.
BAR-024 therefore gives a failed square-free pair. This proves BAR-041 and
REF-043 with the stated grammar-specific scope. \(\square\)

\subsection{Proof of BAR-042}
\label{proof:BAR-042}

For \(2\le t<u\), put \(d=u-t\) and \(k=2^d-1\). Every balanced population
prime at \(m\ge9\) is greater than seven, so BAR-023's generic criterion
applies. If \(p\) divides both \(C_t\) and \(C_u\), then
\[
2^{E_t}\equiv2^{E_u}\equiv-3\pmod p,
\qquad E_u=2^dE_t-5k.
\]
Consequently
\[
-3\equiv(-3)^{2^d}32^{-k}\pmod p.
\]
Multiplication by \(32^k\) and division by the unit \(-3\) give
\[
32^k\equiv(-3)^{2^d-1}=-3^k\pmod p,
\]
because \(k\) is odd. Thus \(p\mid R_d=3^k+32^k\). Also
\[
0<R_d<2\cdot32^k=2^{5k+1},
\]
which proves (M48.1).

For candidate pair \(i<j\), the square-free product of balanced primes
hitting both coordinates divides \(R_{t_{m,j}-t_{m,i}}\). Every such prime
is at least \(2^{b_m}\), so its count is at most the corresponding summand
in (M48.2). Taking the union over all pairs proves (M48.2), conservatively
overcounting primes that hit three or more coordinates.

Every prime outside \(J_m\) has signature weight at most one. There are only
\(r_m+1\) such binary vectors. If (M48.3) holds, two primes outside \(J_m\)
share a signature; BAR-024 converts that duplicate into a failed
square-free balanced pair.

Finally, if \(r_m\le m^a\) and
\(\Delta_m\le(1/2-\varepsilon)m\), then (M48.2) is at most
\[
\binom{r_m}{2}
\frac{5(2^{\Delta_m}-1)+1}{b_m}
=O\!\left(m^{2a-1}2^{(1/2-\varepsilon)m}\right).
\]
BAR-041's inspected prime-counting consequence gives
\(|\mathcal P_m|=\Omega(2^{m/2}/m)\). Their ratio is
\(O(m^{2a}2^{-\varepsilon m})\to0\), so (M48.3) holds eventually. This
proves BAR-042 and REF-044 with the stated short-span scope. \(\square\)

\subsection{Proof of BAR-043}
\label{proof:BAR-043}

Let \(p>7\) hit levels \(t_0<t_1<\cdots<t_h\), put
\(d_i=t_i-t_0\), and let \(q=\gcd(d_1,\ldots,d_h)\). BAR-042 applied to
each pair \((t_0,t_i)\) gives
\[
x^{k_i}=-1\pmod p,
\qquad
x=3\cdot32^{-1},
\qquad
k_i=2^{d_i}-1.
\]
The identity
\[
\gcd(k_1,\ldots,k_h)=2^q-1=:k
\]
follows by repeated Euclidean reduction of the exponents. Write
\[
s_i=\frac{k_i}{k}
=1+2^q+\cdots+2^{q(d_i/q-1)}.
\]
Each \(s_i\) is odd and \(\gcd(s_1,\ldots,s_h)=1\). Choose integers
\(z_i\) with \(\sum_i z_i s_i=1\). Modulo two this equality implies that
\(\sum_i z_i\) is odd. Therefore
\[
x^k
=\prod_i(x^{k_i})^{z_i}
=(-1)^{\sum_i z_i}
=-1\pmod p.
\]
This proves \(p\mid R_q\), including the required negative sign.

The \(h\) numbers \(d_i/q\) are distinct positive integers, so their maximum
is at least \(h\). Hence \(q\le\lfloor\Delta_m/h\rfloor\).
For each \((h+1)\)-subset of levels, the square-free product of all balanced
primes hitting the subset divides its \(R_q\). Every such prime has at least
\(b_m\) bits, and
\[
\operatorname{bitlength}(R_q)
\le5(2^{\lfloor\Delta_m/h\rfloor}-1)+1.
\]
Taking the union over at most \(\binom{r_m}{h+1}\) subsets proves (M49.4);
moving the common denominator outside the sum only enlarges the integer
upper bound.

Every prime outside \(K_{m,h}\) has signature weight at most \(h\), giving
exactly the Hamming-ball capacity (M49.1). Thus (M49.5) implies a duplicate
signature and, by BAR-024, a failed square-free balanced pair.

Now assume \(r_m\le m^a\), \(\Delta_m\le Cm\), and polynomial branch-total
cost. Fix an integer \(h>2C\). If \(r_m\ge h+1\), (M49.4) is
\[
O\!\left(m^{a(h+1)-1}2^{(C/h)m}\right).
\]
Dividing by BAR-041's
\(\Omega(2^{m/2}/m)\) population lower bound gives
\[
O\!\left(
m^{a(h+1)}2^{-(1/2-C/h)m}
\right)\longrightarrow0.
\]
The Hamming-ball capacity is \(O(m^{ah})\), so (M49.5) holds eventually.
If \(r_m\le h\) infinitely often, at most \(2^h\) signatures exist at those
lengths and the conclusion follows directly from the same population lower
bound. This proves BAR-043 and REF-045 with the stated exact-family and
linear-span scope. \(\square\)

\clearpage

The M50 publication projection
\path{schemas/m50-finite-threshold-summary-v1.json} freezes 16 source schemas
and 26 family-relative threshold rows. Its canonical summary SHA-256 is
\texttt{\seqsplit{1fb6185f73b4bc2243dc2f339c1e823d7c849acd7bf33ef5f288af4baa9d00b3}}.
It is a review index over the existing M31--M46 evidence, not a new
experiment or claim promotion.

The exact reproduction commands are:

\begin{verbatim}
python scripts/validate_foundation.py
python scripts/generate_korean_claim_appendix.py --check
python scripts/generate_m50_finite_threshold_summary.py --check
python scripts/check_m50_finite_threshold_summary.py
python scripts/check_publication.py
python -m unittest discover -s tests -v
python -m compileall -q python scripts tests
cargo fmt --all --check
cargo clippy --workspace --all-targets --all-features -- -D warnings
cargo test --workspace --all-features
$mosefAppData = Join-Path (Get-Location) 'tmp/dotnet-appdata'
New-Item -ItemType Directory -Force $mosefAppData
$env:APPDATA = $mosefAppData
dotnet restore verification/csharp/MosefVerifier.csproj `
  --configfile verification/csharp/NuGet.Config `
  --packages .packages/nuget
dotnet build verification/csharp/MosefVerifier.csproj `
  --no-restore --configuration Release
python scripts/check_baseline_differential.py
python scripts/run_m2_separator_search.py `
  --n-max 500 --base-max 20 --exponent-max 20
python scripts/check_m2_separator_differential.py
python scripts/run_m3_semismooth_search.py `
  --n-max 500 --base-bound 5 --smooth-bound 8 `
  --cofactor-bound 3 --collision-bound-max 20
python scripts/check_m3_semismooth_differential.py
python scripts/run_m4_difference_cover_search.py `
  --order-bound 8 --candidate-max 12 `
  --modulus-max 200 --construction-bound 200
python scripts/check_m4_difference_cover_differential.py
python scripts/run_m5_multigroup_search.py `
  --modulus-max 700 --base-max 32 `
  --parameter-max 32 --exponent-max 12

The searches use no random seed. The randomized theorems are validated by
exact successful-residue enumeration in EXP-0003 and exact parameter
enumeration in EXP-0006 rather than by unrecorded random runs. EXP-0007
through EXP-0019 are deterministic combinatorial or syntax
enumerations. Large raw
datasets are not required; scripts, bounds, compact records, canonical
vectors, commits, and hashes reconstruct the reported observations. EXP-0020
through EXP-0048 use the same deterministic finite-enumeration policy. The
stable rendered artifact is \path{output/pdf/mosef-paper.pdf}.
